   179	
   180	\subsection{The controllers leave memory unused}
   181	\label{sec:results:idle-memory}
   182	
   183	Before the routes, one measurement that reframes all of them. Our controller
   184	keeps every session's permit for its lifetime, so its pool occupancy is a
   185	policy output, not a constraint. It runs at a median of 49.2\% of the pool on
   186	the base configuration, and the median number of sessions queueing for
   187	admission is 66. The configuration that finishes fastest---the repeat of that same static cap
   188	of four, at 209.1 minutes---runs at 71.9\%, and its first run at 70.2\%. Occupancy
   189	and running requests track each other reliably across the arms we have (the two
   190	columns in \texttt{occupancy.csv} rank the twenty-three arms we have almost
   191	identically, Spearman $\rho=0.95$), but
   192	running count does \emph{not} order wall clock: \texttt{horizon}=2 runs 1.25
   193	requests against the base arm's 1.14 (the all-samples mean, the column
   194	\texttt{occupancy.csv} carries; \S\ref{sec:model} uses the busy-conditional
   195	one) and finishes 15 minutes slower. That gap is
   196	what the next subsection is about.
   197	
   198	The controller believes it is nearly full. Its own decision log records the
   199	projection it admits against---the largest of measured occupancy and the summed
   200	contexts of live sessions, plus a reserve for their growth---at a median of
   201	89{,}073 tokens against a budget of 96{,}360, i.e.\ 7.6\% free. Its measured
   202	occupancy at the same instants is 49{,}875 tokens: 48.2\% of that budget, or
   203	50.8\% of the pool, is idle. The
   204	projection is 1.79$\times$ the truth on this configuration. Across the
   205	sixteen controller runs that finished on this trace and logged projections the
   206	ratio runs from 1.32 to 1.90 with a median of 1.78, and from 1.32 to 4.11 with
   207	the same median once the second trace's controller arm is included
   208	(\texttt{projection.csv} records each set, its size and its median; the second
   209	trace is the 4.11). So 1.79 is this arm's value, not a constant of the design,
   210	and we name the set because the range depends on it---over every finished run in
   211	that file, including the flash-crowd and 25-session families, it is 1.01 to
   212	4.11 with a median of 1.58. (Runs still in flight are excluded from both
   213	figures: their controller logs are still growing, and one of them reads 1.95
   214	today.) Medians here are
   215	the upper of the two middle values, the convention \S\ref{sec:results:attribution}
   216	uses throughout. Two terms produce the gap: charging every
   217	live session its full context, when a parked session's KV is evictable and
   218	therefore absent from the engine's count, and the growth reserve, which alone
   219	is 26{,}762 tokens, 28\% of the budget.
   220	
   221	Two things follow, and we are careful about the second.
   222	
   223	The first is that the controllers under-use the pool, and that this is a
   224	property of their estimates rather than of the hardware: the base
   225	configuration holds nearly half the pool idle (48.2\% of its budget, 50.8\% of\
   226	the pool) with 66 sessions queued, because
   227	it believes it has 7.6\% free when it has 48.2\%. Any account of why it is slow
   228	that does not mention this is incomplete.
   229	
   230	We then asked what actually stops it, by instrumenting the admission decision.
   231	The path is a conjunction: a session is authorized only if the SLO valve is
   232	open, the round gate is not full, and its projected footprint fits what remains
   233	of the budget. Over the base run's 8{,}196 blocked cycles---cycles with a
   234	session waiting and none admitted---the valve accounts for 6.8\%, the round gate
   235	for none, and a projection already above budget for 38.4\%.
   236	
   237	The remaining 54.8\% are cycles where the controller selected no candidate at
   238	all, and here we are explicit about the limit of what we can resolve. The
   239	controller is single-flight by default: it authorizes at most one session per
   240	two-second cycle and waits for that session to begin a round before authorizing
   241	the next. Whether that gate held or whether a candidate was tried and did not
   242	fit is \emph{not distinguishable from our logs}---the controller does not record
   243	it---so we do not attribute the bucket to either. We bounded it by running that arm: the same configuration with single-flight
   244	disabled. Two things came out of it, and the second is the one that matters.
   245	
   246	The bound first. With the gate off, the residual bucket---which can then only
   247	be \emph{candidates that did not fit}, since the gate cannot be holding---falls
   248	from 54.8\% of blocked cycles to 39.7\%, against 44.0\% that are over budget
   249	outright. So the single-flight path accounts for roughly fifteen points, and
   250	the headroom estimate remains the largest single cause. (Both arms' numbers
   251	are rows of \texttt{gate\_attrib.csv}, which now includes the \texttt{sf=0}
   252	arm; an earlier revision quoted 39.9 and 43.5 for the second pair, values that
   253	appeared in no data file.)
   254	We report what did \emph{not} move, because it is the check we wrote down for
   255	this probe before running it. The two aggregate indicators barely did:
   256	admitting cycles go from 2.35\% to 2.44\% and blocked cycles from 93.3\% to
   257	93.2\%, so on those two the flag looks like it changed nothing. What moves is
   258	the composition \emph{within} the blocked cycles---which is the quantity the
   259	bound above rests on---and it moves well outside the replicate spread, the
   260	repeat of the base arm reading 58.3\% remainder against the original's 54.8\%.
   261	So the probe supports the decomposition and not the claim that disabling the
   262	gate makes the controller admit appreciably more, which is what we say above
   263	it buys 28 minutes for 3.5\,pp rather than a win.
   264	
   265	The second thing is that disabling it does not make the controller win.
   266	\texttt{sf=0} finishes in 263.3 minutes at 86.3\% SLO against the base run's
   267	291.3 and 89.8\%: it buys 28 minutes for 3.5\,pp, and its signed distance from
   268	the front moves from $-1.24$ to $-0.55$---onto the tie band from below it, not
   269	above it. Its admission rate is essentially unchanged (2.44\% of cycles admit, against
   270	2.35\%). So the gate was a real cost and removing it moves the arm along the
   271	front rather than past it, which is the same trade every other route in this
   272	section makes.
   273	
   274	Across the eight \sys{} variants that left controller logs with gate state---the
   275	set is named in \texttt{gate\_attrib.csv}, and it is eight rather than nine
   276	because the ninth, \texttt{sf=0}, is the probe we report separately just above,
   277	and because \texttt{foyfix} and the two AIMD arms run a different controller and
   278	log no gate state---the projection is over budget in a median 32.2\% of blocked
   279	cycles (19.5\% to 53.6\%) and the valve in a median 32.7\% (6.8\% to 67.9\%).
   280	Both medians move with the set, which is why we name it: over every completed
   281	run in that file---forty-two of them---they are 30.6\% and 5.3\%. One
   282	convention note, because it moves
   283	these numbers: we take the median as the upper of the two middle values when
   284	the count is even (\texttt{v[len(v)//2]}, which is what
   285	\texttt{gen\_gate\_attrib.py} computes and what the file's
   286	\texttt{eight\_median} line records), not the mean of the two; on eight arms
   287	that difference is 0.4\,pp on the projection and 6.7\,pp on the valve. The round gate is
   288	off by default and binds in only two of the three arms that turn it
   289	on---\texttt{rg2} at 29.3\% and \texttt{rg3} at 18.0\% of blocked cycles,
   290	while \texttt{rg4}, the loosest of the three, stays at 0.6\% like every arm
   291	that leaves it off---so the gate
   292	binds only where it is set tightly enough to.
   293	We also record what this accounting omits, because it changes the picture: the
   294	controller has a starvation guard that bypasses the budget entirely once the
   295	oldest waiter has waited 240\,s, cooling down for 300\,s. On the base run it
   296	accounts for 24.8\% of all admissions (51 of 206), and while the projection is
   297	over budget it is the \emph{only} way in---of 3{,}700 such cycles, nine admitted,
   298	and all nine were forced. It is therefore the guard, not the budget test, that
   299	sets the floor on how fast this controller can admit.
   300	
   301	The second is that under-use does \emph{not} order the outcomes, so it is not
   302	the whole explanation either. \texttt{horizon}=1 and \texttt{cap3}+HiCache sit
   303	at the same median occupancy (57.8\% of the pool) and the same mean running
   304	count (1.52 against 1.51 on the busy-time measure of \S\ref{sec:model}), and
   305	the cap is 19 minutes faster. \texttt{hz0} runs
   306	at 64.1\%, higher than that cap, and is 54 minutes slower. Occupancy buys
   307	running requests reliably---\texttt{occupancy.csv}'s occupancy and
   308	running-count columns track
   309	each other across all twenty-three arms---but converting running requests into wall
   310	clock is not simply the per-step cost of \S\ref{sec:model}. Decomposing that
   311	19 minutes into its idle and busy components, 12.6 of them are idle time---%
   312	65\%---and only 6.8 are busy; the two runs' step costs differ by 1.2\,ms
   313	(29.2 against 28.0), about 4\%. A parked session is one the controller has
   314	authorized but not yet released, so the same policy that sets the running count
   315	also sets a large part of the idle time. Both
   316	effects are real; on this workload the unused-memory one is larger for the arms
   317	that finish worst, and the step-cost one is what separates the arms that finish
   318	close together.
   319	
   320	\subsection{What the two mechanisms explain, arm by arm}
   321	\label{sec:results:attribution}
   322	
   323	We have offered two mechanisms, and the honest thing is to say what each one
   324	covers. For every controller arm we took the static cap closest to it in wall
   325	clock and compared their occupancy and their step cost. An arm is attributed to
   326	the unused-memory mechanism if it runs fewer than 92\% of its comparison's
   327	requests, and to the step-cost mechanism if it runs a comparable number---%
   328	within 8\%---at a measurably higher cost (more than 8\% higher $\tau_1$).
   329	Everything else is a residual. The rule reads the running count and
   330	$\tau_1$ only; it does not consult occupancy, so the ``unused memory'' label
   331	is licensed by the running count alone---though the three arms it lands on also
   332	sit well below their comparison's occupancy (54.5, 56.2 and 62.1 against
   333	74.3\%), which is why we let the name stand. The comparability clause is load-bearing and we
   334	state it because an earlier version of this table did not enforce it, which
   335	classified every arm that ran \emph{more} requests than its comparison as
   336	step-cost purely because $\tau$ rises with the running count.
   337	
   338	\begin{center}
   339	\small
   340	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
   341	\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}
   342	  \toprule
   343	  Arm & Occ. & $\overline{R}$ & $\tau_1$ & Attribution \\
   344	  \midrule
   345	  single-flight off   &  54.5 & 1.455 & 28.9  & unused mem. \\
   346	  \texttt{horizon}=1  &  57.8 & 1.524 & 29.2  & residual \\
   347	  \sys{} base         &  49.2 & 1.353 & 28.7  & residual \\
   348	  \texttt{horizon}=2  &  54.7 & 1.439 & 33.6  & $\tau$ high, not matched$^\ddagger$ \\
   349	  \texttt{horizon}=0  &  64.1 & 1.666 & 35.7  & $\tau$ high, not matched$^\ddagger$ \\
   350	  \texttt{bres}       &  56.2 & 1.481 & 29.5  & unused mem. \\
   351	  \texttt{breshz1}    &  61.4 & 1.632 & 36.2  & $\tau$ high, not matched$^\ddagger$ \\
   352	  no gate, no resv.   &  62.1 & 1.678 & 31.5  & unused mem. \\
   353	  running gate$=$4    &  62.3 & 1.689 & 32.2  & $\tau$ high, not matched \\
   354	  all throttles off   &  39.2 & 1.675 & 34.8  & $\tau$ high, not matched \\
   355	  AIMD, retuned       &  66.6 & 1.706 & 34.3  & residual \\
   356	  AIMD, paper params  &  40.3 & 1.357 & 32.4  & step cost$^\ddagger$ \\
   357	  \bottomrule
   358	\end{tabular}
   359	\end{center}
   360	
   361	$^\ddagger$ marks runs measured while three or more other engine instances shared
   362	the host for at least an hour (\S\ref{sec:repro}). In this table the flag bites
   363	harder than in Table~\ref{tab:runs}, because the classification is made on
   364	$\tau_1$, which is exactly the column co-tenancy inflates. Three of the five
   365	$\tau$-high rows carry it, and so does the one step-cost attribution the
   366	procedure makes---AIMD at its published parameters. That row is flagged by the
   367	window but not measurably damaged: its $\tau_2$, which does not touch the
   368	sampler, is 24.4\,ms, inside the clean family's range. We report it as flagged
   369	rather than calling it clean, because the window is what the audit establishes
   370	and the absence of damage is an inference from a different column. The two
   371	$\tau$-high rows that are not flagged (\texttt{running gate}=4 is a watch,
   372	all-throttles-off is clean) are the ones whose elevation no window explains.
   373	
   374	4 of the 12 rows are attributed: three to unused memory (\texttt{single-flight
   375	off}, the charge-free base, and no-gate-no-reservation), one to step cost, five
   376	have a $\tau$ no
   377	matched comparison can confirm, and three are residuals---their occupancy and
   378	their step cost both sit close to their comparison cap's, and their shortfall
   379	is real but we cannot say from these measurements which mechanism produced it.
   380	Two limits on what that buys, both of which we want read before the number is
   381	quoted. First, the comparison is a static cap matched on \emph{wall clock},
   382	and the arms that finish near 260 minutes are matched against \texttt{cap5},
   383	whose SLO is 27.1\%---so the classification describes an arm relative to a
   384	wall-clock peer, not relative to the static \emph{front}, and it cannot say
   385	why the arm fails to beat the front. Second, the three unused-memory rows run
   386	fewer requests than their comparison, which is a description of what they did,
   387	not a demonstration that doing otherwise would have won; \S\ref{sec:results}
   388	is the section that establishes the loss, and this table only sorts it.
   389	We publish the table rather than a summary because a summary would claim more
   390	than we have---and because ``two mechanisms explain the negative result''
   391	would be the wrong summary. We also flag what changed here, since an earlier
   392	version of this table claimed four attributions from eleven rows and that was
   393	an artifact of two errors at once: the comparability clause above was not
   394	enforced in the code, and six arms had no $\tau_1$ because the run list behind
   395	this table was incomplete. The generator is now the only source for these rows
   396	(\texttt{attribution.csv}), it enforces the clause, and it carries the
   397	co-tenancy flag rather than having it written in by hand---which is how the
   398	earlier version came to mark two clean runs.
   399	
   400	\subsection{Route 1: the accounting base}
   401	
   402	Our controller's projection (Eq.~\ref{eq:proj}) charges a session for its full
   403	context even while it is parked in a tool wait, when its KV is evictable and
   404	therefore absent from the engine's reported occupancy. We argued this was a
   405	double charge and removed it, admitting on engine telemetry alone.
   406	
   407	The charge is not double, and removing it does what the argument predicted on
   408	the engine side: median occupancy rises from 49.2\% to 56.2\% of the pool and
   409	the busy-hour running count from 1.35 to 1.48, and the round-1+ prefix hit
   410	rate falls only from 80.6\% to 79.7\%, so no eviction spiral occurred. The run
   411	also finishes 25.4 minutes \emph{earlier} (265.9 against 291.3).
   412	
   413	It is nonetheless not a win. The SLO falls from 89.8\% to 85.9\%---3.90\,pp---
   414	and the static front itself falls 3.81\,pp over those same 25.4 minutes, so the
   415	arm keeps its place relative to the envelope to within 0.09\,pp while moving
   416	25 minutes along it: it slides, at the front's own slope, rather than
   417	crossing. What makes that a test rather than a coincidence is what it would
   418	have taken to cross: at 265.9 minutes the front sits at 87.2\%, so the arm
   419	would have had to give up no more than 2.6\,pp of SLO instead of 3.9, and it
   420	did not.
   421	
   422	We are careful not to rest anything on the 0.09\,pp. It is not a resolution
   423	claim, and the base configuration's own four runs show why: against this same
   424	front they sit at $-1.24$, $-1.73$, $-1.49$ and $+0.18$\,pp, a 1.9\,pp spread.
   425	That is the scale this statistic moves on, and it means the charge-free arm's
   426	distance from any \emph{one} of those runs is not resolvable---measured
   427	against the second of them it is 0.40\,pp \emph{better}, against the fourth
   428	1.5\,pp worse. The slope comparison is the part that survives, and it is the
   429	part the conclusion rests on.
   430	
   431	We want to be explicit that this is a revision. Our earlier measurement of this
   432	route had the arm 4.2 minutes \emph{slower} and 6.0\,pp worse, and we read the
   433	extra concurrency and the extra step cost---$\overline{R}$ up 1.35 to 1.48,
   434	$\tau_2$ up 23.8 to 31.3\,ms---as cancelling. That measurement was one of the
   435	co-tenancy-flagged runs (\S\ref{sec:repro}), and its $\tau_2$ was the inflated
   436	term: measured alone the same arm reads 24.8\,ms, so the second factor is
   437	$+$4.2\% rather than $+$31.5\%, and busy-time throughput
   438	($\overline{R}/\tau$) rises 5.1\% where the flagged measurement had it
   439	falling 17\%. Nothing cancels. The occupancy half of the argument
   440	(49.2\% to 56.2\%) is a volume measurement and was never in doubt. We keep the
   441	route in the negative column, now on the stronger ground that the highest-leverage
   442	knob the mechanism argues for moves the arm along the front rather than over it,
   443	and we record the correction here rather than in a footnote because it is the
   444	one place in this paper where a re-run changed a conclusion's reasoning rather
   445	than its precision.
   446	
   447	We note that this claim had already been falsified once by source inspection:
   448	SGLang's \texttt{token\_usage} subtracts evictable blocks, so a parked
   449	session's prefix is genuinely not counted, and charging for it reserves
   450	capacity that really will be needed back. The end-to-end measurement agrees
   451	with the source reading.
   452	
   453	\subsection{Route 2: the growth reserve}
   454	
   455	Sessions grow by a median of 2.7K new tokens per round (\texttt{input\_len}
   456	over rounds $\ge 1$ in the trace), so the controller reserves
   457	capacity for the next \texttt{horizon} rounds. We swept the horizon over
   458	$\{0,1,2,3\}$ and the reserve is not a free parameter to be minimised:
   459	Fig.~\ref{fig:horizon} shows the response is non-monotone. Removing the
   460	reserve entirely costs 12.6\,pp of SLO, and a reserve of two rounds is
   461	15 minutes \emph{slower} than three. The optimum is at one round, and there it comes closest to the
   462	static front---0.93\,pp below it, inside the tie band---and that single point is
   463	the one this paper spends the most words on, because it does not reproduce: the
   464	repeat of this configuration lands 5.6\,pp below the line
   465	(\S\ref{sec:results}). Read the sweep as a shape, not as a result at its
   466	minimum.
   467	
   468	\subsection{Route 3: the hard ceiling}
   469	
   470	Lowering the budget fraction is the crudest available knob, and on the two
   471	settings we swept it does not behave like the others: budgets of 0.85 and 0.90
   472	finish in 333.9 and 315.9 minutes at 89.9\% and 87.7\% SLO, which is $-2.24$
   473	and $-4.22$\,pp from the envelope at those wall clocks (\texttt{budget\_sweep.csv}),
   474	against $-0.93$ and $-1.24$ for the two nearest arms at the 0.95 the rest of
   475	the paper uses. So this knob slides the point \emph{below} the front rather
   476	than along it. We report it
   477	only for completeness, since it adds no mechanism.
   478	
   479	\subsection{An independent adaptive controller}
   480	\label{sec:results:aimd}
   481	
   482	Every controller above is ours, which invites the objection that the negative
   483	result is a statement about one design rather than about adaptive admission.
   484	We therefore implemented a second, mechanistically different controller: an
   485	AIMD window over an agent-level admission set, following the control law of
   486	the congestion-based system we compare against in \S\ref{sec:related}. The
   487	window grows additively while cache occupancy sits below a low watermark, is
   488	cut multiplicatively when occupancy exceeds a high watermark \emph{and} the
   489	prefix hit rate falls below a threshold, and holds otherwise. Cuts pause the
   490	youngest sessions at their next step boundary; growth resumes paused sessions
   491	first. Every input is a runtime gauge---occupancy and hit rate from the
   492	engine, nothing from the trace---and the window acts through the same named
   493	permits as \sys{}.
   494	
   495	It does not beat the static front either, at either parameter setting
   496	(Table~\ref{tab:runs}). At the values reported in that paper's evaluation it
   497	finishes in 364.3 minutes at 81.4\% SLO, $11.2$\,pp below the envelope; the
   498	same arm with its retuned watermarks finishes in 264.6 minutes at 52.8\%,
   499	$34.3$\,pp below (\texttt{table1.csv}). The retuned setting is also one of the most
   500	volatile runs we report: its segment-to-segment SD is 13.2\,pp of SLO, with
   501	segment values swinging between 37 and 72. Two runs are more volatile
   502	(\texttt{cap4} at 15.8 and the all-throttles-off arm at 14.4), and no other arm
   503	that keeps its throttles on is as bad---which is what a control loop oscillating
   504	around a capacity cliff looks like.
   505	
   506	We report two things honestly about this comparison. First, this is our
   507	reimplementation of a published control law, not the authors' system, and a
   508	reimplementation of any controller can underperform its paper. We built it to
   509	the published law, gave it the same permit interface and the same 2\,s cycle
   510	as \sys{}, and did not tune it beyond the two documented settings; but the
   511	caveat stands. Second, both controllers stall. At its paper parameters this one pinned its
   512	window at one session, admitted nothing, and left the engine idle for over an
   513	hour. Our own controller does the same thing more briefly: in 4 of the 11
   514	\sys{} runs a scan finds windows in which the engine was idle, 85 or more
   515	sessions were waiting, the SLO valve was open and the controller issued no
   516	permit at all. Three of those runs have a single such window each---170.5, 173.2
   517	and 191.5\,s, 0.9--1.1\% of the run's wall clock---and the arm that disables
   518	every throttle has nine of them, together 12\% of its wall clock (\texttt{stall.csv} states
   519	the criterion; an earlier draft quoted 150--175\,s and 0.9\% for all four,
   520	which no version of the criterion reproduces). The remaining runs, including
   521	the best one, show no such window. Nothing is logged
   522	as an error and the run recovers on its own.
   523	
   524	We traced one of those windows to a
   525	code path that explains it: single-flight admission considered only the head of
   526	the FIFO queue, and when the head was a session too large to fit the remaining
   527	budget it was never admitted, never left the queue, and blocked every smaller
   528	session behind it. That is the code every run in this paper executed. The fix
   529	in our repository---scan the queue, still admit at most one per cycle---is
   530	\emph{not} in the artifact that produced these results, so the numbers here
   531	predate it and we do not claim them for it. We are also explicit that this is
   532	\emph{a} path, not a proven cause:
   533	the audit is a log signature, and the same signature can be produced by other
   534	closed gates. What we can say is that the signature is present in a third of
   535	our \sys{} runs and absent from all of our static caps, which have no
   536	admission decision to get stuck in.
   537	
   538	That is the honest version of a finding we first reported too strongly. An
   539	intermediate draft of this paper called it a deadlock and said the
   540	configuration that produced our best result was the one that deadlocked on its
   541	repeat. Neither holds: the windows are minutes, not hours, and they clear on their
   542	own, and the configuration that produced our best result never showed one. Nor
   543	do they move any verdict we report---0.9--1.1\% of the wall clock in each of
   544	the three runs that has a single one, and in the all-throttles-off arm 12\% of
   545	a wall clock that is already the slowest in the table. We keep them because the mechanism is real and its size
   546	is the point: on a pool that holds four median sessions, a controller's failure
   547	mode is a silent stall, not graceful degradation, and it took a repeat run to
   548	see it.
   549	
   550	\subsection{Route 4: execution granularity}
   551	\label{sec:results:gate}
   552	
   553	The first three routes all assume that what a controller can set is \emph{how
   554	many sessions are resident}. A static cap sets the same quantity, so it is
   555	unsurprising that they land in the same place. The fourth route attacks the
   556	assumption instead: because a large fraction of live sessions are parked at
   557	any instant, a cap on \emph{resident} sessions leaves the engine idle whenever
   558	all residents happen to be parked. Capping the \emph{running-request} count
   559	instead should keep the engine busy while leaving the resident count high.
   560	
   561	The permit design supports this directly: a paused session finishes its
   562	current round and blocks before its next one, and resumes when the controller
   563	re-admits it. We implemented it as a gate on the engine's own
   564	\texttt{num\_running\_reqs} and swept the gate over $\{2,4\}$. Only the
   565	gate-of-four arm ran to 998 of the trace's 999 steps; the gate-of-two arm
   566	reached 770 and is not used, so what we report below is a single comparison
   567	against the no-gate arm rather than a sweep.
   568	
   569	It does not work, and the measurement says why. Against the no-gate arm, a
   570	gate of four moved mean running requests from 1.33 to 1.30 and mean live
   571	sessions from 3.25 to 3.03: it tightened both, by 2\% and 7\%, rather than
   572	pinning the one it aims at. That is what we
   573	observe, and we state it as the observation it is rather than deriving a bound
   574	we cannot support. A live session spends most of its time parked in a tool wait
   575	(the ratio of mean running requests to mean live sessions is 0.33 to 0.64
   576	across the twenty-three arms, \texttt{occupancy.csv}'s \texttt{running\_mean}
   577	over \texttt{runs.csv}'s \texttt{conc}), so pausing live sessions removes
   578	capacity that was not running in the first place---but that ratio is an average over a run, not a
   579	bound on any instant, and the running count can in principle reach the live
   580	count. Both repairs fail: without a
   581	constraint on admission, a paused session is re-admitted on the next cycle and
   582	blocks for at most one 2\,s cycle; with one, the policy is a session cap again.
   583	The obstacle is granularity---\texttt{paused} takes effect at round
   584	boundaries, while pinning an instantaneous count requires control the
   585	permit mechanism does not have.
   586	
   587	The measured outcome matches: the gate moved running concurrency from 1.33 to
   588	1.30 and live sessions from 3.25 to 3.03. It tightened rather than pinned, and
   589	a tightening is another cap.
   590	
   591	\begin{figure}[tb]
   592	  \centering
   593	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig6_horizon.pdf}
   594	  \caption{The growth reserve is not a free parameter. Removing it costs
   595	  12.6\,pp of SLO; two rounds is 15 minutes slower than three. The optimum at
   596	  one round comes closest to the static front, 0.9\,pp below it---and is the
   597	  one point here that does not reproduce on a repeat (it lands 5.6\,pp
   598	  below).
   599	  The other three settings were each run once, so the shape is better
   600	  supported than any single point on it.}
   601	  \label{fig:horizon}
   602	\end{figure}

     1	\section{The Same Measurements on a Second Workload}
     2	\label{sec:xtrace}
     3	
     4	Everything so far is one trace family, and a negative result drawn from one
     5	family invites the objection that the family is the finding. We ran the same
     6	accounting, a static sweep from a cap of two to a cap of eight, and the same
     7	controller on a second replay:
     8	117 real Claude agent sessions, 390 rounds, the same model, the same engine and
     9	the same 101{,}432-token pool. One difference has to be stated up front,
    10	because it bears on the paper's central variable: \emph{this trace was run
    11	entirely without a host tier}. Its static points, its envelope and its
    12	controller are all device-only. That makes the comparison internally fair---both
    13	sides of the front are the same configuration---but it means this workload
    14	tests the negative result and not the tier mechanism, and it leaves an
    15	alternative account of the controller's failure standing: it failed here on the
    16	configuration where \S\ref{sec:model} says added concurrency is expensive. The two workloads differ in ways that matter for
    17	this paper, which is why we chose it. Its sessions are smaller (median prompt
    18	18{,}809 tokens against 23{,}187) and its tool waits are far longer at the
    19	median: over round transitions---same session,
    20	adjacent rounds, each session's last round excluded because it has no
    21	successor---the gap is 20.2\,s against 0.34\,s at the median---about 60$\times$---and
    22	39.9\,s against 15.8\,s at the mean, which is 2.5$\times$
    23	(\texttt{toolwait.csv} carries both traces on this definition). The 29.1\,s
    24	that appears in \texttt{elasticity\_x.csv} is the mean over all 390 rows
    25	including each session's last round, and is not comparable with the main
    26	trace's 15.8\,s; we use the transition definition throughout §\ref{sec:xtrace}. So the engine is idle far more of the
    27	time, and the running counts are lower.
    28	
    29	\subsection{What transfers, and what does not}
    30	
    31	\begin{figure}[tb]
    32	  \centering
    33	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig8_transfer.pdf}
    34	  \caption{Realization against the running count on both traces, each
    35	  normalized to its own trace's reuse bound. Device-only, the main trace falls
    36	  from 92.5\% to 36.4\% and the second from 74.8\% to 57.5\%; with a host
    37	  tier the main trace's runs stay at 72.8--92.5\%. The second trace is lower
    38	  throughout---interpolating the main trace's line at the second trace's
    39	  running counts gives a 17-point gap at the lowest and 23 at the
    40	  highest---and it declines more gently, which is what ``the direction
    41	  transfers and the level does not'' looks like on this axis.}
    42	  \label{fig:transfer}
    43	\end{figure}
    44	
    45	\paragraph{The reuse accounting transfers in direction, not in level.} The
    46	second trace plans a higher reuse bound than the first---86.3\% of its prompt
    47	tokens against 75.5\%---and realizes less of it, at a lower device occupancy
    48	(Fig.~\ref{fig:transfer}).
    49	Realization falls from 74.8\% to 57.5\% as the running count rises from 1.08 to
    50	1.50, so the direction of \S\ref{sec:model} survives. What does \emph{not}
    51	survive is the sharpness. On the main trace the decline is a threshold: the
    52	per-unit slope is $-10.4$, $-40.6$, then $-157.1$ and $-127.6$ points per unit
    53	as the running count passes 1.53, which is the cliff \S\ref{sec:criterion}
    54	measures. Here the slope stays gentler through the same region---$-31.2$,
    55	$-36.8$, $-28.4$, $-93.0$ and $-67.6$---and the two steep numbers at the end
    56	sit on spans of 0.017 and 0.074 of running count, where a difference between
    57	two runs starts to read as a trend. Taken as a whole the sweep loses 74.8--57.5\%
    58	of this trace's bound between $\overline{R}$ 1.08 and 1.50 at $-40.9$ points
    59	per unit. We do compare the two
    60	traces' overall slopes, which is the one slope comparison the data supports
    61	because both are taken over their full measured ranges
    62	(\texttt{slope.csv}): the device-only family runs from 92.5\% to 36.4\% over
    63	$\overline{R}$ 1.000--1.842, a whole-range slope of $-66.8$ points per unit,
    64	and this trace from 74.8\% to 57.5\% over 1.08--1.50, a slope of $-40.9$.
    65	So per unit of running count, the main trace loses its bound about one and a
    66	half times as fast over its range ($-66.8$ against $-40.9$)---but the
    67	comparison is between ranges of different length and
    68	different absolute level, and we do not lean on it.
    69	What this trace does establish is that the \emph{level} does not transfer, and
    70	we treat that as a bound on what we can
    71	claim: it realizes \emph{less} at 15.8\% median device occupancy than the
    72	main trace does at 22.4\%---the two numbers are the lightest cap on each trace
    73	(\texttt{realhr\_cap2} realizing 74.8\% and \texttt{cap1} realizing 92.5\%), both
    74	medians of \texttt{sglang:token\_usage} with means of 13.9\% and 25.2\%
    75	(\texttt{reuse\_x.csv}, \texttt{occupancy.csv})---so realization is not a
    76	function of occupancy alone and a
    77	rule of the form ``the pool collapses above $x$\% occupancy'' is false on our
    78	own data. Occupancy is instantaneous; reuse depends on whether a prefix
    79	survives from one round to the next, which is a history the instantaneous
    80	number does not carry. We do not measure that history here, and we say so
    81	rather than fitting the two traces to a common curve.
    82	
    83	\paragraph{The elasticity criterion transfers in sign, not in place.} On both
    84	traces the criterion plays the same role, and it is the modest one
    85	\S\ref{sec:criterion} settles on: it \emph{rules out} the workload-side
    86	story. The controller on this trace runs at a busy-time mean of 1.223, which
    87	falls inside the span its own static caps cover (1.08 to 1.50)---more
    88	precisely inside the cap3$\to$cap4 segment, where
    89	$\varepsilon(\tau_1) = -0.28$ over $\overline{R}$ 1.16--1.29, so its failure is
    90	again not explained by the criterion.
    91	What the completed sweep adds is that this trace does approach the criterion's
    92	\emph{expensive} branch, later and more gently than the main trace: above
    93	$\overline{R} \approx 1.29$ the model-consistent elasticity turns positive and
    94	touches one in the first of the three segments there ($+1.0$, then $+0.18$ and
    95	$+0.87$ as the sweep reaches 1.50), while the main trace crosses one at 1.53
    96	with $+1.15$ and stays above it. So the direction is not a property of one
    97	family; the location is. We report the point estimates and not a threshold, because a $\pm10\%$
    98	perturbation of $\tau_1$---inside the range our repeat pairs show---carries
    99	each of those three segments' bounds across zero \emph{and} across one. The per-request
   100	measure is no steadier ($+0.45$, $-1.25$, $+0.73$). And the controller never
   101	operates where the branch turns expensive: 1.22, below 1.29 and below the main
   102	trace's 1.53 alike.
   103	
   104	We also record what the noise does, because it is the same weakness the whole
   105	paper has. Recomputing each segment's $\varepsilon$ with $\tau_1$ perturbed
   106	by $\pm10$\%---against the 0.3--1.1\% our clean repeat pairs differ by in
   107	$\tau_1$ (\texttt{decomp.csv}; one anomalous pair reaches 42.5\%), so the
   108	perturbation is generous rather than conservative---gives bands of
   109	$[-5.20, +0.41]$ for cap2$\to$cap3,
   110	$[-2.17, +1.61]$ for cap3$\to$cap4, $[-1.25, +3.26]$ for cap4$\to$cap5,
   111	$[-14.07, +14.43]$ for cap5$\to$cap6 and $[-3.33, +5.07]$ for
   112	cap6$\to$cap8. Every one of them crosses zero, and the three at the end cross
   113	one as well. So at that perturbation no segment's sign is resolvable, and the
   114	turn the paragraph above describes is a property of the point estimates rather
   115	than a boundary we have located---which is a bound on that paragraph, not a
   116	reprieve from it.
   117	
   118	\paragraph{The controller's verdict transfers, weakly.} \sys{} finishes the
   119	second trace in 75.6 minutes, against 66.2 for the fastest static run on it
   120	(the cap of eight), so \sys{} is 14.3\% slower (14.26\% at full precision).
   121	We do not report that as a loss. The repeat pairs we have vary by 1.8--4.7\%
   122	in wall clock (\texttt{replicates.csv}), and this trace gives us two of its
   123	own on the same clock (\texttt{repeat\_spread.csv}): the cap of four repeats
   124	to within 1.6\%, and \sys{} itself to within 0.02\%, the latter accompanied
   125	by 0.03 points of realization on the volume axis. The gap is more than
   126	eight times the wider of those, so it is not repeat noise on either side of
   127	it---but two pairs cannot bound the variability of the \emph{other}
   128	configurations in the comparison, and run-to-run spread on this machine
   129	reaches 22.0\% across the
   130	other configurations and tiers we have measured (\texttt{repeat\_spread.csv};
   131	0.02--6.08\% across the ten repeat \emph{groups} those two traces give us---%
   132	four runs in one of them, two in each of the rest---the spread between the
   133	groups themselves being the reason we report a range and not a number).
   134	So the honest reading is the one we have used throughout: at the threshold
   135	this paper uses, it did not win on
   136	either axis.
   137	Its signed distance from the second trace's
   138	static envelope is $+0.40$\,pp at $10$\,s on
   139	the SLO axis---inside the $\pm1.0$\,pp tie band, which is defined on that
   140	axis---and the repeat of the same configuration reads $+0.40$ as well
   141	($+0.4012$ and $+0.4017$ at full precision), so this is not a single
   142	measurement standing alone---and $-0.14$\,pp on the cache-hit axis, against the 14.9 points that
   143	separate this trace's own six static caps on that axis. That axis is
   144	threshold-dependent here, and in the opposite direction from the main trace's:
   145	at $5$\,s this trace's caps separate much more (the cap of eight scores
   146	$78.2$\% against the cap of two's $99.0$\%) and the controller sits
   147	$+1.87$\,pp above the envelope---outside the band, a win on that axis---whereas
   148	on the main trace none of the cleanly measured controllers reaches the front at
   149	$5$\,s, and one appears above it at
   150	$20$\,s in a single measurement whose repeat lands below the line
   151	(\S\ref{sec:discussion}). We report both readings rather than the one
   152	that gives a single answer, and we report one more thing the repeat settled:
   153	\emph{that single crossing does not reproduce}. The same configuration run
   154	again reads $+0.86$\,pp at $5$\,s---inside the band---against the first run's
   155	$+1.87$, while at $10$\,s the two agree to three decimals ($+0.40$ each). So
   156	the one place in this paper where a controller appears above the front is also
   157	a place where a second run puts it back inside the tie band. (The envelope and the
   158	elasticity below are both built from all six static caps the sweep now has;
   159	the three caps the sweep added after the first version of this comparison---the
   160	caps of five, six and eight---are the reason the last three segments exist
   161	at all.)
   162	The SLO number is nearly uninformative, and we flag that rather than let it
   163	stand as a measurement. At the three caps the envelope was originally built
   164	from, every point on this trace scored 99.5--100.0\%, so the axis could not
   165	discriminate at all. The three the sweep has since added do separate---the cap
   166	of eight scores 93.8\% against the cap of two's 100.0\%---so the axis is not
   167	flat after all, but its whole spread over six static caps is 6.2 points against
   168	a $\pm1.0$\,pp tie band, and \sys{} sits at 100.0\% on it.
   169	
   170	\subsection{What this section does and does not buy}
   171	
   172	It buys a negative result that is not a property of one trace family: on a
   173	second workload, with a different session-size distribution and a
   174	tool-wait distribution about 60$\times$ longer at the median, the same controller
   175	still does not beat the same kind of static cap, and the reuse degradation
   176	still appears. It costs us the transportability of the threshold. On the main trace the
   177	criterion narrowed the search by excluding the workload-side explanation and
   178	put the turn of the point estimates at $\overline{R} \approx 1.53$; on this
   179	trace the same measurement turns positive above 1.29. What we cannot do is
   180	\emph{locate} either turn---the $\pm10\%$ perturbation puts every segment's
   181	bounds across zero on both traces---which is the caveat
   182	\S\ref{sec:criterion} already states for the main trace, now with a second
   183	trace behind it. The controller is below both turns (1.22), so the criterion
   184	does not explain its failure on either trace. We
   185	report both, and we note that the second workload is closer to the regime
   186	where adaptive control \emph{should} pay---lower concurrency, much longer idle
   187	gaps---and the controller still did not win there.
     1	\section{Load Dependence}
     2	\label{sec:load}
     3	
     4	A negative result about adaptivity invites the obvious objection: adaptivity
     5	pays when conditions \emph{change}, and a single stationary trace is the one
     6	case where a static cap should be near-optimal. We agree. What we test here is
     7	the weaker thing---two separate constant-load tiers---and we also ran the
     8	stronger one, a trace whose load moves mid-run, whose baselines co-tenancy made
     9	unusable; \S\ref{sec:discussion} reports both what this section leaves open and
    10	why that experiment has no result to give.
    11	
    12	We run the same controller and the same cap family on a 25-session tier. The
    13	throttle configuration is reversed: with all safety throttles disabled, the
    14	25-session tier runs \emph{51\% faster} (58.4 to 28.8 minutes) at a cost of
    15	only 2.4\,pp of SLO, while on the 200-session tier disabling the same
    16	throttles costs 42\% (291.3 to 415.0 minutes) and 43.7\,pp of SLO. One switch,
    17	two regimes, opposite signs, both around 40--50\%
    18	(Fig.~\ref{fig:loadtier}). No single scalar setting is optimal across both.
    19	
    20	We flag the generation gap rather than let the figure imply more than we ran.
    21	The two 200-session numbers are current; the two 25-session numbers are from an
    22	earlier harness revision, and we never ran the all-throttles-off arm on the
    23	25-session tier under the current one. So the reversal is a direction we
    24	measured across two harness generations, not a same-generation paired
    25	comparison, and a reviewer should read it that way. The same-generation
    26	25-session measurement we do have is in Table~\ref{tab:tier25}, and it does not
    27	contain that arm.
    28	
    29	This is the regime in which adaptive control should win, and we now have the
    30	same-generation measurement that earlier drafts of this paper were missing.
    31	Four arms on the 25-session tier, all under the current harness
    32	(Table~\ref{tab:tier25}): static caps of two, three and four finish in 32.4,
    33	24.2 and 23.6 minutes at 92.8\%, 91.2\% and 79.2\% SLO, and \sys{} finishes
    34	in 27.6 minutes at 92.8\%.
    35	
    36	\begin{table}[tb]
    37	  \caption{The 25-session tier, all four arms under the current harness.
    38	  This is the same-generation comparison earlier drafts of this paper were
    39	  missing; \texttt{cap4} and the \sys{} arm were run for this revision. The
    40	  segment SD here is over six segments, not the ten Table~\ref{tab:runs} uses
    41	  for 999-step runs, so the two tables' SD columns are not comparable.}
    42	  \label{tab:tier25}
    43	  \centering
    44	  \small
    45	  \setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
    46	  \begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
    47	    \toprule
    48	    Policy & Wall (min) & Conc. & SLO (\%) & Seg.\ SD (pp) \\
    49	    \midrule
    50	    cap2          & 32.4 & 1.95 & 92.8 & 11.0 \\
    51	    cap3          & 24.2 & 2.77 & 91.2 &  5.1 \\
    52	    cap4          & 23.6 & 3.39 & 79.2 & 21.4 \\
    53	    \sys{}        & 27.6 & 2.47 & 92.8 &  7.1 \\
    54	    \bottomrule
    55	  \end{tabular}
    56	\end{table}
    57	
    58	The finding is a trade, not a defeat, and it is the opposite of what the
    59	200-session tier shows. Against the same-generation envelope---which runs from
    60	\texttt{cap4} at (23.6, 79.2) through \texttt{cap3} at (24.2, 91.2) to
    61	\texttt{cap2} at (32.4, 92.8)---\sys{} sits $+0.9$\,pp above the line. A
    62	static cap of three is still 12\% faster in wall clock, but it gives up
    63	1.6\,pp of SLO to do it, so neither dominates the other. On this tier the
    64	controller is a different point on the front rather than a dominated one,
    65	which is exactly what the 200-session tier denies it.
    66	
    67	We are careful about the size, and about the yardstick. $+0.9$\,pp is
    68	measured against a straight-line interpolation between static points, not
    69	against a switching policy anyone ran, so the defensible statement is that
    70	\sys{} is not dominated by any static point we measured on this tier---not
    71	that it beats what a static mix could achieve. On 125 requests one request is
    72	worth 0.8\,pp, so the margin is about one request. The tier's
    73	segment-to-segment SD (5.1 to 21.4\,pp) is not the right yardstick: segment
    74	volatility is not run-to-run uncertainty. The right one is the replicate spread
    75	we have---which, across the five configurations we repeated, is 0.5, 0.8, 1.0,
    76	3.9 and 5.1\,pp (\texttt{replicates.csv}). Against the two tightest of those $+0.9$\,pp is about a replicate
    77	wide; against the three widest---which belong to the configurations that decide
    78	our headline elsewhere---it is inside one. We quote the tightest pair
    79	because it is the least favourable to us, not because it is the whole
    80	distribution. What we will defend is the weaker claim: on the
    81	low-load tier the controller is not worse, and the same switch that is worth
    82	$+51\%$ there costs $-42\%$ at high load. A controller that is competitive
    83	where load is light and dominated where load is heavy is the pattern this
    84	paper set out to explain, and it is the pattern we measured.
    85	
    86	The second finding is the more useful one. The signal an adaptive controller
    87	would need is available---the number of sessions waiting for admission is four
    88	times larger on the 200-session tier than on the same-generation 25-session arm
    89	(median 66 against 16, maximum 135 against 25; the earlier-harness 25-session
    90	arms of \S\ref{sec:load} read 9--11, which is where the 6--7$\times$ of an
    91	earlier draft came from, and we quote the same-generation pair)---but its
    92	\emph{interpretation} is not obvious. At the 25-session tier the controller
    93	holds sixteen sessions back while running one; that backlog is manufactured by
    94	the throttling itself rather than
    95	evidence that demand has slackened. Reading it correctly requires combining
    96	instantaneous backlog with progress through the run, which is a stateful
    97	computation a cap cannot perform. We regard this as the most promising
    98	direction we did not finish, and we are explicit that we did not finish it.
    99	
   100	\begin{figure}[tb]
   101	  \centering
   102	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig5_loadtier.pdf}
   103	  \caption{The same throttle switch, two load tiers, opposite signs. The
   104	  25-session bars are from an earlier harness revision and are shown as a
   105	  direction, not a magnitude---the current-harness 25-session comparison is in
   106	  Table~\ref{tab:tier25} instead. The 200-session bars are current. The two
   107	  panels have independent axes, so the bars are not comparable in height
   108	  across panels---the annotations give each panel's own change.}
   109	  \label{fig:loadtier}
